An ultrasensitive photoelectrochemical platform
page=1 block=1278677872 (x=1 y=62) width=493.000000 height=13.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=9
for quantifying photoinduced electron-transfer
page=1 block=1278677872 (x=1 y=90) width=477.000000 height=13.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=10
properties of a single entity
page=1 block=1278677872 (x=1 y=118) width=286.000000 height=13.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=10
Wei Ma , Hui Ma, Yue-Yi Peng, He Tian * and Yi-Tao Long*
page=1 block=341796579 (x=1 y=152) width=325.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Understanding the photoinduced electron-transfer process is of paramount importance for realizing ef?cient solar energy
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=179) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
conversion. It is rather dif?cult to clarify the link between the speci?c properties and the photoelectrochemical
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=190) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=8
performance of an individual component in an ensemble system because data are usually presented as averages because
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=202) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of interplay of the heterogeneity of the bulk system. Here, we report a step-by-step protocol to fabricate an ultrasensitive
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=213) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
photoelectrochemical platform for real-time detection of the intrinsic photoelectrochemical behaviors of a single entity
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=225) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
with picoampere and sub-millisecond sensitivity. Using a micron-thickness nanoparticulate TiO2-?lmed Au ultramicro-
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=236) width=493.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
electrode (UME) as the electron-transport electrode, photocurrent transients can be observed for each individual
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=248) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
dye-tagged oxide semiconductor nanoparticle collision associated with a single-entity photoelectrochemical reaction. This
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=259) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
protocol allows researchers to obtain high-resolution photocurrent signals to quantify the photoinduced electron-transfer
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=270) width=493.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
properties of an individual entity, as well as to precisely process the data obtained. We also include procedures for
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=282) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
dynamic light scattering (DLS) analysis, transmission electron microscopy (TEM) imaging and collision
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=293) width=493.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=11
frequency–concentration correlation to con?rm that the photoelectrochemical collision events occur at an unambiguously
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=305) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
single-entity level. The time required for the entire protocol is ~36 h, with a single-entity photoelectrochemical
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=316) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=8
measurement taking <1 h to complete for each independent experiment. This protocol requires basic nanoelectrochemistry
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=328) width=493.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
and nanotechnology skills, as well as an intermediate-level understanding of photoelectrochemistry.
page=1 block=807657332 (x=1 y=339) width=413.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Introduction
page=1 block=825936265 (x=1 y=376) width=52.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=19
KeyLaboratoryofAdvancedMaterials,SchoolofChemistryandMolecularEngineering,EastChinaUniversityofScienceandTechnology,Shanghai,
page=1 block=1164107853 (x=1 y=670) width=493.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=15 wordint.max=15
People’s Republic of China. *e-mail: tianhe@ecust.edu.cn; ytlong@ecust.edu.cn
page=1 block=711327356 (x=1 y=680) width=262.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

page=1 block=68377659 (x=1 y=703) width=16.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=13
NATURE PROTOCOLS |VOL 14|SEPTEMBER 2019|2672–2690|www.nature.com/nprot
page=1 block=1661123505 (x=243 y=703) width=251.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
https://doi.org/10.1038/s41596-019-0197-8
page=1 block=825249556 (x=336 y=24) width=153.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=15
Photoelectrochemical reactions have gained much attention among researchers over the past decades,
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=394) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
mainly because of their powerful utility in solar energy conversion1–3. Photoinduced electron transfer
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=403) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
is an essential step in the conversion of light into electric power in photoelectrochemistry4,5, also
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=415) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
frequently called photo-driven electrochemical reactions by chemists6,7. Among the most ef?cient
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=426) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
photoelectrochemical reactions are those involving light absorption by wide-band-gap semiconductor
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=440) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
nanoparticles and molecular adsorbates, which result in the occurrence of electron-transfer reactions
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=451) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
at the semiconductor interface that are the basis for photoelectric conversion in photovoltaic devi-
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=463) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
ces2,8,9. Therefore, understanding the photoinduced electron-transfer process is of paramount
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=472) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=8
importance for realizing ef?cient conversion of solar energy. A range of analytical methods can be
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=486) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
used to study photoelectrochemical properties, such as time-resolved photocurrent measurement,
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=497) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
photovoltage measurement and electrochemical impedance spectroscopy4–8. These techniques are
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=506) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
capable of investigating the physicochemical properties and photoelectric performance of different
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=520) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
types of photoelectrochemical processes; however, substantial enhancements of the design of highly
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=532) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
ef?cient photoelectrochemical devices remain a great challenge for the following reasons10,11. The
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=541) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
inability to quantify the structure–activity relationship because of the interplay of structural and
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=555) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
functional heterogeneity among individual entities is a major barrier12,13. The intrinsic properties
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=564) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
are masked in traditional photoelectrochemical measurements, producing averaged data caused by
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=578) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
measurement of large ensembles of molecules or billions of nanoparticles. This could result in
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=589) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
overlooking a subpopulation minority species with outstanding activities14–17. In addition, the sur-
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=598) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
rounding medium and assembly process are important factors that affect conversion ef?ciencies.
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=612) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
Therefore, thorough insights into the photoelectrochemical performance of a single entity would
page=1 block=883151184 (x=112 y=623) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6

NATURE PROTOCOLS
page=2 block=1550207152 (x=7 y=3) width=117.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
NATURE PROTOCOLS|VOL 14|SEPTEMBER 2019|2672–2690|www.nature.com/nprot
page=2 block=1864230087 (x=1 y=707) width=251.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
2673
page=2 block=1168019749 (x=478 y=707) width=16.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=13
allow the roles of nanoparticles and dye molecules to be quanti?ed, uncovering the intrinsic prop-
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=30) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
erties of photoinduced transfer under realistic reaction conditions.
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=41) width=255.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
In contrast to studies on a bulk system, single-entity electrochemistry has emerged as a unique tool
page=2 block=311239812 (x=123 y=53) width=371.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
for exploring the structural and kinetic features of individual entities during stochastic collision
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=64) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
measurements at a UME surface, avoiding any inhomogeneous averaging and broadening effects18–20.
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=73) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Many examples of single-entity collision experiments have been reported in this ?eld, such as elec-
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=87) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
trocatalysis21–23, blocking24,25 and electrochemical stripping26–29. For example, the electrocatalytic
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=96) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
behavior of individual entities has been studied as they collide on a relatively inert UME, resulting in
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=110) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
current transients due to the catalytic ampli?cation–mediated electron-transfer process19–23. Quan-
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=119) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
titative information about individual nanoparticles, such as size, morphology and concentration, can
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=133) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
be obtained by measuring the faradic charge associated with the electrochemically destructive process
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=144) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
of direct oxidation of each colliding nanoparticle26,27. Recently, this technique has also been utilized
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=153) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
to investigate the electron-transfer dynamics of individual nanoparticles at the UME interface30–33.
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=165) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Therefore, stochastic collision electrochemical measurements offer direct electron-transfer informa-
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=179) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tion at interfaces and provide valuable evidence in studying the intrinsic electrochemical activities of
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=190) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
an individual entity18–30. In addition, they also have advantages that include rapid and sensitive
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=199) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
measurement, low cost, lack of requirement for labeling, high throughput and low sample con-
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=213) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
sumption31–35. If we could use single-entity electrochemistry to study the photoelectrochemical
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=222) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
reaction, we could solve the ensemble problem described above. However, the key bottleneck in
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=236) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
applying single-entity electrochemistry to photoelectrochemical measurement is the need for real-
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=248) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
time recording of the ultrafast photoinduced electron-transfer data36. That is, the electron-transfer
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=257) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
process of a photoelectrochemical reaction occurs on a sub-picosecond timescale and cannot be
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=270) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
electrochemically resolved because of the limitations of current electrochemical instruments. Current
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=282) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
photoinduced electron-transfer research is mainly based on ultrafast time-resolved transient spec-
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=293) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
troscopy techniques for tracking the intermediate states of the electron-transfer process in photo-
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=305) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
electrochemical reactions on picosecond and femtosecond timescales37–39.
page=2 block=311239812 (x=112 y=314) width=283.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Development and applications of the protocol
page=2 block=2093010349 (x=112 y=350) width=197.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
A useful strategy for achieving in situ electrochemical measurement of photoinduced electron transfer
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=362) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
at the single-entity level is to fabricate a micron-thickness nanoparticulate TiO2 ?lm–coated Au UME
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=374) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
(TiO2@Au UME) as an electron-transport electrode. At this electrode, photoinduced electrons
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=385) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
undergo a trap-limited diffusion process in the TiO2 ?lm, causing a substantial extension of electron
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=397) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
transit time. Photoinduced electrons move through the TiO2 ?lm to the Au UME electrode in a
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=408) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
multiple-trapping process. Upon visible light absorption by N719 dye molecules, the incident photons
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=419) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
are converted into electron–hole pairs, and a gradient electrical potential is frequently generated to
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=431) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
cause the separation of electron and hole. Photoexcited electrons are subsequently injected from the
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=442) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
excited state of N719 into the semiconductor conduction band of neighboring ZnO or TiO2 nano-
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=454) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
particles. When individual N719@TiO2 or N719@ZnO entities stochastically collide with TiO2@Au
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=465) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
UME, the electrons are transferred into the nanoparticulate TiO2 ?lm. The electrons then travel
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=477) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
through the TiO2 ?lm to reach the Au UME electrode. The photoexcitation, electron separation and
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=488) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
injection are usually ultrafast and take place on a picosecond timescale. Therefore, in our photo-
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=500) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
electrochemical measurements, the rate-determining steps are the collision of a nanoparticle with the
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=511) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
TiO2@Au UME and/or the electron diffusion process inside the TiO2 ?lm.
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=523) width=288.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
To elucidate the electron-transfer dynamics of the collision process, we studied the photoelec-
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=123 y=534) width=371.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
trochemical behaviors of individual dye-tagged oxide semiconductor nanoparticles colliding with a
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=546) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
bare Au UME. No current signal was observed under illumination in this case (Supplementary Fig. 1).
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=557) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
This demonstrates that the collision occurs in a very short time at bare Au electrode surfaces and is
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=568) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
faster than the acquisition time of a single-point electrochemical measurement. In this study, we
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=580) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
performed single-entity photoelectrochemical experiments using a low-noise electrochemical
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=591) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=9
instrument at a sampling rate of 100 kHz. The internal low-pass Bessel ?lter of the ampli?er was set
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=603) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
to 5 kHz. This means the collision process takes place in <66 ?s, as estimated by instrumental
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=614) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
resolution. To capture the transient signal, we needed to substantially extend the residence time of the
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=626) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
nanoparticle on the electrode or greatly slow the electron transfer in the TiO2@Au UME. Therefore,
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=637) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
we designed a micron-thickness semiconductor ?lm of nanoparticulate TiO2 and coated the surface
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=649) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
of an Au UME with it so that photoinjected electrons proceed with trap-limited diffusion in the TiO2
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=660) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
?lm. As a result, the typical duration of electron transit in TiO2 ?lm was considerably increased,
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=672) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
allowing the achievement of successful real-time detection of single-entity photoelectrochemical
page=2 block=2088445230 (x=112 y=683) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7

semiconductor nanoparticles during a collision at a TiO2@Au UME under visible light (? > 450 nm, Xe lamp). When
page=3 block=1365008457 (x=112 y=254) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
visible light shines, dye molecules are excited from their ground state (S0) to a higherenergy state (S*).The oxidized
page=3 block=1365008457 (x=112 y=261) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
dye molecule (S+) is again regenerated by electron donation from the reduced species (R) in the electrolyte,
page=3 block=1365008457 (x=112 y=271) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
generating the oxidized species (O). A/D, analog-to-digital; NP, nanoparticle; Vis, visible light.
page=3 block=1365008457 (x=112 y=283) width=315.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
behaviors. Owing to a trap-mediated electron diffusion process in TiO2 ?lms, we observed sub-
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=306) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
millisecond photocurrent transients associated with single-entity photoelectrochemical reactions.
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=317) width=370.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Using this approach, we developed an ultrasensitive photoelectrochemical platform for real-time
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=123 y=329) width=371.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
photocurrent detection of individual dye-tagged oxide semiconductor nanoparticles with picoampere
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=340) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
and sub-millisecond sensitivity40,41 (Fig. 1). In particular, the high resolution and sensitivity of this
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=349) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
technique could enable a wide range of potential applications for single-entity photoelectrochemical
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=363) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
measurements, for example, in dye-sensitized solar cells, photoelectrochemical water splitting and
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=375) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
photocatalysis systems.
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=386) width=87.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
This strategy greatly enhances the possibilities of applying single-entity electrochemistry to a broad
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=123 y=398) width=371.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
range of photoelectrochemical measurements. Based on high-resolution photoelectrochemical mea-
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=409) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
surements, our platform can be used for quantifying the photoinduced electron-transfer process of a
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=420) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
single event, enabling electron-transfer dynamics analysis at a semiconducting TiO2 ?lm. This may
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=432) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
have considerable impact in the ?eld of intrinsic performance of photoelectrochemical devices. It will
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=443) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
certainly help to achieve an improved understanding of the electron-transfer process at the semi-
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=455) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
conductor interface, where the photoelectrochemical reaction processes take place.
page=3 block=1671179293 (x=112 y=466) width=317.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Limitations and alternative methods
page=3 block=1609124502 (x=112 y=500) width=155.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Owing to the use of TiO2@Au UME as an electron-transport electrode, an energy level match
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=512) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
between dye/metal oxide nanoparticles and the TiO2 semiconductor interface is critical for ef?cient
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=524) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
electron collection42. To obtain high ef?ciency for injection of dye molecules into the semiconductor,
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=533) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
a good overlap between the excited-state levels of the dye molecule and the conduction band levels of
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=547) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
the semiconductor is required43. Among the different types of dyes, ruthenium(II) (Ru(II))-based
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=556) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
systems—such as N719, N3, and black dye—are the most popular for use with the liquid iodide/
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=569) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
triiodide (I?/I3?) electrolyte44,45 and zinc(II) porphyrins have reached high ef?ciency when coupling
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=578) width=382.000000 height=10.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
to cobalt(II)/(III) redox shuttles46. Moreover, a number of metal-free dyes47 can also be used in our
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=590) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
photoelectrochemical measurement system. However, not all metal oxide nanoparticles and dyes will
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=604) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
work with TiO2@Au UME because of mismatched energy levels. In addition, considering the extreme
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=615) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
sensitivity of individual event signals, testing the effects of the ?lter and ampli?er of a low-noise
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=627) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
electrochemical instrument should be performed as a ‘best practice’ standard in single-entity pho-
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=638) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
toelectrochemical studies to minimize the electronics’ effects on measurement results48,49. Such effects
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=647) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
can potentially limit the general applicability of this method. To assist researchers in choosing the
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=661) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
most suitable preparation approach for metal oxide-?lmed UMEs, alternative methods50–54, including
page=3 block=1144068272 (x=112 y=670) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
their relative advantages and limitations, are outlined in Supplementary Table 1.

NATURE PROTOCOLS|VOL 14|SEPTEMBER 2019|2672–2690|www.nature.com/nprot
page=4 block=758348212 (x=1 y=707) width=251.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
2675
page=4 block=817978763 (x=478 y=707) width=16.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFAE+AdvOTcb88df00 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=13
The approach described here also has some other limitations. (i) Nanoparticle suspensions should
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=123 y=30) width=371.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
consistently remain monodispersed during the entire photoelectrochemical measurement, which can
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=41) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
be achieved by controlling the concentrations of nanoparticle suspensions and the electrolyte solution
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=53) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
at the (approximately) picomolar and millimolar levels, respectively. (ii) The applied potential should
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=64) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
remain in the range from +800 to ?600 mV versus Ag/AgCl to prevent any damage to the TiO2 ?lm
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=75) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
on the Au UME. (iii) Incident light intensity should be <200 mW. Higher light intensity causes an
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=87) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
increase in current background noise due to local thermal drift.
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=99) width=245.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
In this protocol, we describe step-by-step procedures for the fabrication of TiO2@Au UMEs, the
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=123 y=110) width=371.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
preparation of monodispersed dye-tagged oxide semiconductor nanoparticle suspensions and the
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=122) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
subsequent procedures to monitor the photoelectrochemical behaviors of individual entities in real
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=133) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
time, process data and con?rm single-nanoparticle events. As a model system, we use the N719 dye
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=144) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
molecules27 and TiO2/ZnO semiconductor nanoparticles5 as the photosensitizers and charge trans-
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=153) width=382.000000 height=10.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
port carriers, respectively. As demonstrated, this platform offers picoampere and sub-millisecond
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=167) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
sensitivity in terms of the current magnitude and duration of individual collision events at a
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=179) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
TiO2@Au UME interface, allowing the quantitative measurement of the photoinduced electron-
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=190) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
transfer processes of a single entity. We strongly believe this protocol could pave the way for the
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=202) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
fabrication of high-performance photoelectrochemical devices by quantifying the contributions of
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=213) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
individual components with single-entity photoelectrochemical measurements.
page=4 block=1578009262 (x=112 y=225) width=301.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

page=7 block=231756373 (x=112 y=286) width=230.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Piranha solution
page=7 block=1786294176 (x=112 y=317) width=65.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENONKF+AdvOTb65e897d.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
To make piranha solution, mix 30% (wt/wt) H2O2 and 98% (wt/wt) H2SO4 at a ratio of 3:7 (vol/vol).
page=7 block=793331940 (x=112 y=329) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Mix the solution in an open glass container when needed. !CAUTION Mixing of H2O2 and H2SO4 is
page=7 block=793331940 (x=112 y=340) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
highly exothermic and can be explosive, especially if mixed with any organic solvent. Wear personal
page=7 block=793331940 (x=112 y=352) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
protective equipment and work in a fume hood. !CAUTION Do not add H2SO4 to H2O2, as this will
page=7 block=793331940 (x=112 y=363) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
cause intense and rapid heating of the resulting mixture, potentially splashing this strongly oxidizing
page=7 block=793331940 (x=112 y=375) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
solution. ! CAUTION Excess solution should be disposed of in an appropriate manner. Piranha solution
page=7 block=793331940 (x=112 y=386) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
must be left in an open container in a safe area to cool for several hours before storing for disposal. Once
page=7 block=793331940 (x=112 y=398) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
cooled to room temperature, the solution can be transferred to a closed glass container for waste storage.
page=7 block=793331940 (x=112 y=409) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Equipment setup

page=7 block=275266973 (x=112 y=450) width=76.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENONKF+AdvOTb65e897d.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
All three-electrode electrochemical experiments are performed using a CHI660E electrochemical
page=7 block=1069716895 (x=112 y=461) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
workstation. A glass electrochemical cell is set up on a cell stand, with three electrodes inserted into
page=7 block=1069716895 (x=112 y=473) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the cell: a TiO2@Au UME (10 µm) as the working electrode, a platinum wire counter electrode and an
page=7 block=1069716895 (x=112 y=484) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
Ag/AgCl reference electrode (Fig. 2, top left). Add 6 mL of ferrocene electrolyte solution to the
page=7 block=1069716895 (x=112 y=495) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
electrochemical cell and purge the solution with argon for 10 min through Te?on tubing. Maintain
page=7 block=1069716895 (x=112 y=507) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the solution under argon atmosphere during subsequent electrochemical measurements.
page=7 block=1069716895 (x=112 y=518) width=338.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Photoelectrochemical cell
page=7 block=1521083627 (x=112 y=547) width=98.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENONKF+AdvOTb65e897d.B wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
All single-entity photoelectrochemical measurements are performed using an Axopatch 200B ampli?er
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=558) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
in combination with a xenon lamp with a ?lter to cut off the wavelengths of light <450 nm under an
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=569) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
argon atmosphere throughout the experiment in a Faraday cage at 25 °C (Fig. 2, right). An enclosed
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=581) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
quartz electrochemical cell is set up on a cell holder, with two electrodes inserted into the cell: a
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=592) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
TiO2@Au UME and an Ag/AgCl reference electrode (Fig. 2, bottom left). The TiO2@Au UME is
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=604) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
aligned carefully so that the light beam from the xenon lamp passes through the center of the TiO2@Au
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=615) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
UME. To maintain the stability of the de?ned reference potential over prolonged measurement periods
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=627) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
and avoid contamination-induced effects from the reference electrode55, the Ag/AgCl wire should be
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=636) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
placed at a distance of a few millimeters from the TiO2@Au UME electrode and used routinely in a
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=650) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
simpli?ed two-electrode format. Add 3 mL of electrolyte solution (I?/I3? electrolyte, DMSO–water
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=658) width=382.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
solution or DMSO–methanol solution) to the photoelectrochemical cell. Purge the solution by bubbling
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=673) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
argon for 10 min through the Te?on tubing before measurements. Considering the high photocatalytic
page=7 block=1770642014 (x=112 y=684) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

activity of ZnO and TiO2 nanocrystals under ultraviolet light, unless otherwise stated, visible light
page=8 block=109228794 (x=112 y=30) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
(a xenon lamp with wavelength >450 nm) is used. After ?ltering, the spectral composition <450 nm is

completely cut off (Supplementary Fig. 2). In addition, the intensity of light in the vicinity of the

TiO2@Au UME should be 100 mW/cm2, which is measured by an Si detector.

Procedure
page=8 block=561959774 (x=1 y=90) width=43.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=19
Fabrication and characterization of TiO2@Au UMEs ? Timing ~24 h
page=8 block=2110756088 (x=112 y=109) width=294.000000 height=8.000000 fontname=ENOBCL+AdvOT5777b7ad wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
1 Clean a borosilicate capillary (length, 10.0 cm; i.d., 1.16 mm; o.d., 2.0 mm) and an Au wire (length,
page=8 block=580871917 (x=112 y=122) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
1.0 cm; diameter, 10 µm) by sonication sequentially in 10 mL of acetone, ethanol and Milli-Q water
page=8 block=580871917 (x=129 y=133) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
in a glass vial in a water bath in an ultrasonic cleaner for 30 min in each solution.
page=8 block=580871917 (x=129 y=145) width=312.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Rinse the borosilicate capillary and Au wire extensively with copious amounts of Milli-Q water. Dry
page=8 block=580871917 (x=129 y=156) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
them in a covered glass vial in an oven at 120 °C for 8 h.
page=8 block=580871917 (x=129 y=168) width=218.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Pull the borosilicate capillary to produce a micropipette with a tip size of dozens of micrometers
page=8 block=580871917 (x=129 y=179) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
using a micropipette puller (two-step pulling, heater level no. 1: 63%; no. 2: 78%).
page=8 block=580871917 (x=129 y=190) width=309.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
2
page=8 block=823723302 (x=112 y=156) width=4.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
3
page=8 block=1714078840 (x=112 y=179) width=4.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
4
page=8 block=1732502545 (x=112 y=213) width=4.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
5
page=8 block=1051876890 (x=112 y=271) width=4.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

Place the microtip vertically at the center of a heated resistor coil and then connect the micropipette
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=339) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
to a vacuum pump with a soft rubber tube. Turn on the vacuum pump and slowly actuate a three-
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=351) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
way stopcock to create a vacuum in the micropipette over 30 min.
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=362) width=251.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
sudden pressure change.
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=385) width=92.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Turn on the micropipette puller. Slowly move the micropipette deeper into the heating coil to
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=397) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
ensure proper sealing as heater level gradually increases from 50% to 70% (Narishige PC-10; seal
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=408) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
length: 5 mm). Turn off the heater and vacuum. Let the microtip cool for 5 min. Remove the
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=420) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
capillary and check the micropipette under a microscope to ensure that it has been sealed with the
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=431) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Au wire inside and without any air bubbles.
page=8 block=989892772 (x=129 y=443) width=166.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
tip from bending and air bubbles from forming during the sealing process.

Use a beveler to polish the microtip on a diamond lapping ?lm (3.0 µm) until the Au wire is

exposed under microscopy. Polish the obtained Au UME on diamond lapping ?lms (1.0 and

0.5 µm) in sequence to obtain a smooth mirror surface. Rinse with ultrapure water and dry the Au

UME for the fabrication of TiO2 ?lm.

CRITICAL STEP Keep the Au UME perpendicular to the diamond lapping ?lms during polishing
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=138 y=534) width=356.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
process to obtain a ?at, smooth surface. Rinse the Au UME surface with Milli-Q water and dry with
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=546) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
nitrogen ?ow after each polishing cycle to remove the Au and glass granule debris attached to the
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=557) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
surface.
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=569) width=28.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=17
Dip a sable paintbrush (size 05) into TiO2 paste A (20 nm) and paint the paste over the surface of
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=580) width=365.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
the Au UME until the surface is uniformly coated. Dry the TiO2 paste in a vacuum oven at 125 °C
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=591) width=365.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
for 8 min. Repeat this painting and drying processes to fabricate several TiO2 paste A layers on the
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=603) width=365.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Au UME. Sequentially, brush-paint the Au UME with the TiO2 paste B (400 nm) and then dry it in
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=614) width=365.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
a vacuum oven at 125 °C for 8 min.

page=8 block=2125238280 (x=138 y=637) width=356.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
UME, keep it in a clean dry cabinet for 3 min to allow the paste to relax and the surface irregularity
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=649) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
to reduce before drying. The thickness of the TiO2 ?lm depends on the number of layers of TiO2

paste A. Typically, ?ve layers of paste A and one layer of paste B correspond to 4- to 5-µm ?lm
page=8 block=2125238280 (x=129 y=672) width=365.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
thickness.

amounts of ethanol and Milli-Q water, and then let it dry under a nitrogen ?ow. Repeat Step 10.

In this protocol, we have described detailed procedures for fabrication of an ultrasensitive photo-
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=535) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
electrochemical platform, which could be widely used for real-time monitoring of the electron-
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=547) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
transfer process of a single entity in various photoelectrochemical systems. Two types of oxide
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=558) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
semiconductor (TiO2 and ZnO) nanoparticles with N719 dye modi?cation were prepared for
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=569) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
intrinsic photoelectrochemical behavior measurements in dye-sensitized solar cells and visible-light
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=581) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
water-splitting systems, respectively. UV-visible and FTIR spectra (Step A(vi, vii) and step 1B(vii) of
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=592) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Box 2) were used to characterize N719 molecules anchored on the surface of ZnO or TiO2 nano-
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=604) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
particles via bidentate carboxylate linkages (Supplementary Figs. 11 and 12). For TiO2@Au UME, a
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=615) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
cross-sectional SEM image shows a compact TiO2 ?lm with a thickness of 4.4 ?m (Fig. 4a). Top-view
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=627) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
SEM images of TiO2@Au UMEs reveal that the desired highly accessible nanocrystalline TiO2
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=638) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
nanoparticulate ?lm has been coated onto the surface of the Au UME. Figure 4b displays the time-
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=650) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
resolved photocurrent responses of TiO2@Au UME as the UV light is turned on and off. In addition,
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=661) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Fig. 4c shows a typical UME voltammogram with a well-resolved limiting current for 1-mM ferro-
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=673) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
cene, 1-mM N719 acetonitrile solution at the TiO2@Au UME surface. An enhanced limiting current
page=15 block=236840983 (x=112 y=684) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5

page=16 block=1087081975 (x=1 y=707) width=251.000000 height=4.000000 fontname=ENOFCK+AdvOTea1a7398 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
2687

was observed under visible-light illumination as compared to the data obtained in dark conditions.
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=30) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
All characterization results demonstrated the successful fabrication of a micron-thickness nano-
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=41) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
particulate TiO2 ?lm with a substantially high electron mobility and photoconductivity.
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=53) width=337.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Current–time curves of individual N719@TiO2 entities colliding with a TiO2@Au UME are col-
page=16 block=680712932 (x=123 y=64) width=371.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
lected in acetonitrile solutions containing 10 mM LiI and 1 mM I2 (Step 19A). Under visible light,
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=76) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
substantial current transients were observed at +600 mV after the injection of the freshly prepared
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=87) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
N719@TiO2 nanoparticles into the electrolyte solution; however, negligible transient signals were
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=99) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
obtained in the dark (Fig. 5a). This indicates the photoinduced electron transfer does not occur
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=110) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
without photoexcitation. Note that no current signal was observed for the neat electrolyte solution
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=122) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
without N719@TiO2 entities, with illumination or without. Moreover, current–time responses
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=133) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
showed a ?at curve (no current spikes) in the absence of I?/I3? redox electrolyte under illumination.
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=142) width=382.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
This is because the I?/I3? redox pair acts as an ef?cient electron donor for N719. There would be no
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=153) width=382.000000 height=10.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
continuing electron ?ow in this case if there were no I?/I3? present in the electrolyte. In addition, no
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=165) width=382.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
current transient was observed for individual bare TiO2 nanoparticles without the N719 dye mod-
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=179) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
i?cation in an amperometric current–time curve (Supplementary Fig. 8), demonstrating that the
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=190) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
collected electrons result from the photo-excited N719 moieties under visible-light illumination.
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=202) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
Figure 5b shows the representative current traces that initially rise quickly and decay at a slower rate
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=213) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
for individual N719@ TiO2 entities. The most population of collision events had a mean duration of
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=225) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
0.4 ± 0.1 ms (Fig. 5c) and a mean amplitude of 21.5 ± 2.0 pA (Fig. 5d). Considering the electron
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=236) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
recombination in a multiple-trapping TiO2 ?lm, the effective number of collected electrons for a
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=248) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
single N719 molecule per typical collision (Ne) at a TiO2@Au UME from the measured transients was
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=259) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
calculated by Ne = Q/neNN719, where Q is the integrated charge of individual events, n is the number
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=270) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
of electrons involved in the photoelectrochemical reaction (1 for N719), e is the elementary charge
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=282) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
and NN719 is the average number of N719 molecules modi?ed onto the surface of a single TiO2
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=293) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
nanoparticle surface (~600; Supplementary Fig. 9). Average integrated charge of individual events was
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=305) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
estimated to be 4.9 ± 1.3 fC from photocurrent transients (Fig. 5e). This allows us to calculate an Ne
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=316) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
value of 51 ± 4 per N719 molecule (Fig. 5f), revealing the intrinsic electron-transfer number of a
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=328) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
single N719 molecule for a typical collision. In this measurement, the TiO2 ?lm is essential to the
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=339) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
successful trapping of the photoinduced electron-transfer process because no collision event could be
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=351) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
observed for N719@TiO2 entities at a bare Au UME (Supplementary Fig. 1). This could be attributed
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=362) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
to instantaneous collisions of individual entities beyond the measurement capability of current
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=374) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
electrochemical instrumentation or high energy dissipation for the transfer of electrons through the
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=385) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
TiO2 semiconductor–Au metal interface56.
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=394) width=163.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
As a parallel study, we also investigated single-entity photoelectrochemistry for N719@ZnO
page=16 block=680712932 (x=123 y=408) width=371.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
nanoparticles suspended in a DMSO–water solution at TiO2@Au UMEs with different TiO2 ?lm
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=419) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
thicknesses (Step 19B). Owing to the varying diffusion and recombination processes of the photo-
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=431) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
injected electrons in the different nanoparticulate TiO2 networks, thickness-dependent current traces
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=442) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
were observed for individual N719@ZnO nanoparticle collisions corresponding to single-entity
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=454) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
photocatalytic water oxidation (Fig. 6a). The current, charge, collision frequency and Ne of individual
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=465) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=4
N719@ZnO nanoparticles as functions of the TiO2 ?lm thickness were plotted (Fig. 6b). A repre-
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=477) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
sentative current–time curve at a 4.4-?m-thick TiO2@Au UME under visible light or in the dark is
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=488) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
shown in Supplementary Fig. 10a. The photocatalytic water oxidation for oxygen evolution was
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=500) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=6
further demonstrated by gas chromatographic results under visible-light irradiation (Supplementary
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=511) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
Fig. 10b)41. Furthermore, the turnover number (TON = Q/neNN719) for oxygen evolution of a single
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=520) width=382.000000 height=9.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
N719 per collision was estimated to be 285 ± 12 at a 4.4-?m TiO2@Au UME (Supplementary
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=534) width=382.000000 height=7.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=7
Fig. 10c). In addition, the measured results of TiO2 ?lm thickness-dependent current traces further
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=546) width=382.000000 height=6.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
demonstrated that a trap-limited electron diffusion process was the rate-limiting step in our single-
page=16 block=680712932 (x=112 y=557) width=382.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=ENOFBH+AdvOT1ef757c0 wordint.min=1 wordint.max=5
entity photoelectrochemical measurement.

Nature Research wishes to improve the reproducibility of the work that we publish. This form provides structure for consistency and transparency 
page=20 block=888655833 (x=2 y=67) width=529.000000 height=5.000000 fontname=AIBFAP+Calibri-Light wordint.min=2 wordint.max=4
in reporting. For further information on Nature Research policies, see Authors & Referees and the Editorial Policy Checklist.
